*(Right page — Folio 149 recto, headpiece engraving: Cartouche with monogram surmounted by cross and flanked by foliage)*

# APPENDIX: SUNDAY THEOTOKIA OF THE EIGHT TONES

*Sunday Resurrection Theotokia of the Eight Tones, at Vespers at "Lord, I have cried," and at the Aposticha.*[^791]

*Tone 1. At "Lord, I have cried":*[^792]
Let us hymn the Virgin Mary, the glory of all the world, who blossomed forth from mankind and bore the Master, the celestial gate, the song of the bodiless hosts, and the adornment of the faithful; for she was shown forth as heaven and the temple of the Godhead: she destroyed the middle wall of enmity, brought in peace, and opened the Kingdom! Having her therefore as the confirmation of our faith, we have the Lord Who was born of her as our Defender: take courage therefore, take courage, O people of God; for He will conquer the enemies, as the Almighty!

*At the Aposticha, Theotokion:*[^793]
Lo, the prophecy of Isaiah is fulfilled; for a Virgin thou hast given birth, and after giving birth thou hast remained even as thou wast before birthgiving; for He Who was born was God, therefore also He made nature anew: but O Mother of God, despise not the supplications of thy servants offered unto thee in thy temple; but as carrying the Tender-hearted One in thy hands, have compassion upon thy servants, and pray that our souls may be saved!

*Tone 2. Theotokion:*[^794]
The shadow of the law passed away when grace came; for even as the bush burned not when it was on fire, so a Virgin thou hast given birth, and a Virgin thou hast remained: instead of a pillar of fire, the Sun of righteousness has shone forth; instead of Moses, Christ the salvation of our souls!

*At the Aposticha, Theotokion:*[^795]
O wonder new, exceeding all ancient wonders! For who has known a Mother who has given birth without a man, and carries in her arms Him Who upholds all creation? It is the counsel of God that has been born, Whom as an Infant having carried in thy hands, O all-pure one, and having maternal boldness toward Him: cease not to pray for those who honor thee, to show mercy and save our souls!

*(Left page — Folio 149 verso, running header: SUNDAY THEOTOKIA OF THE EIGHT TONES)*
*Tone 3. Theotokion:*[^796]
How can we not marvel at thy God-man birthgiving, O all-honored one? For having received no trial of man, O all-immaculate one, thou didst bear in the flesh a Son without a Father, born of the Father without a Mother before all ages, Who underwent no change, nor mingling, nor division, but preserved intact the property of both natures. Therefore, O Mother and Virgin Sovereign Lady, pray Him that the souls of those who orthodoxly confess thee as Theotokos may be saved!

*At the Aposticha, Theotokion:*[^797]
Without seed from the Holy Spirit, but by the will of the Father, thou didst conceive the Son of God, Who exists before the ages from the Father without a Mother, but for our sake came to be from thee without a Father, Whom thou didst bear in the flesh, and didst nourish as an Infant with milk. Therefore cease not to pray, that our souls may be delivered from tribulations!

*Tone 4. Theotokion:*[^798]
David the Prophet, the forefather of God, melodiously proclaimed concerning thee for thy sake unto Him Who worked great things in thee: At Thy right hand stood the Queen! For God Who was well pleased without a father to become man from thee showed thee forth as the Mother and mediatress of life, that He might renew His image corrupted by passions, and having found the sheep that went astray upon the mountains, taking it upon His shoulder, He might bring it unto the Father, and according to His will unite it with the celestial powers, and save the world, O Theotokos: Christ Who has great and rich mercy!

*At the Aposticha, Theotokion:*[^799]
Look down upon the entreaties of thy servants, O all-immaculate one, assuaging the fierce assaults against us, and delivering us from every sorrow; for thee alone do we possess as a firm and sure confirmation, and we have acquired thy protection: let us not be put to shame, O Sovereign Lady, who call upon thee; hasten unto the supplication of those who faithfully cry aloud unto thee: Rejoice, O Sovereign Lady, help of all, joy, protection, and salvation of our souls!

*Tone 5. Theotokion:*[^800]
In the Red Sea the image of the unwedded Bride was prefigured of old: there Moses was the parter of the water, and here Gabriel was the minister of the marvel. Then Israel traversed the deep dry-shod; and now the Virgin has given birth unto Christ without seed. The sea remained impassable after the passage of Israel; the immaculate one remained incorrupt after the birthgiving of Emmanuel...

*(Right page — Folio 150 recto, running header: SUNDAY THEOTOKIA OF THE EIGHT TONES)*
...incorrupt. He Who is and Who was before, manifested as man, O God, have mercy on us!

*At the Aposticha, Theotokion:*[^801]
Thou art a temple and a gate, a palace and a throne of the King, O all-honored Virgin, through whom my Deliverer, Christ the Lord, appeared unto those asleep in darkness, being the Sun of righteousness, wishing to enlighten what He created according to His image by His own hand. Therefore, O all-hymned one, as having acquired maternal boldness toward Him, unceasingly pray that our souls may be saved!

*Tone 6. Theotokion:*[^802]
Who will not bless thee, O Most Holy Virgin? Who will not sing of thy most pure birthgiving? For the only-begotten Son Who shone forth timelessly from the Father, the same came forth from thee, pure one, having ineffably become incarnate: being God by nature, and for our sake having become man by nature, not divided into two Persons, but known in two natures without confusion: pray Him, O pure and all-blessed one, that He have mercy upon our souls!

*At the Aposticha, Theotokion:*[^803]
My Maker and Deliverer, Christ the Lord, O all-pure one, coming forth from thy womb and clothing Himself in me, has delivered Adam from the primal curse. Therefore unto thee, O all-pure one, as truly the Mother of God and Virgin, do we cry aloud unceasingly: Rejoice angelically! Rejoice, O Sovereign Lady, protection, shelter, and salvation of our souls!

*Tone 7. Theotokion:*[^804]
Thou wast known as Mother above nature, O Theotokos, yet didst remain a Virgin above word and understanding, and no tongue can declare the marvel of thy birthgiving; for the conception being all-glorious, O pure one, the manner of birthgiving is incomprehensible: for where God wills, the order of nature is overcome. Therefore, knowing thee to be the Mother of God, we all fervently pray thee: Pray that our souls may be saved!

*At the Aposticha, Theotokion:*[^805]
Beneath thy shelter, O Sovereign Lady, all the earthborn fleeing, do we cry aloud unto thee: O Theotokos, our hope, deliver us from boundless transgressions, and save our souls!

*Tone 8. Theotokion:*[^806]
The King of heaven, through love for mankind, appeared upon earth and lived with men; for from the pure Virgin *(Left page — Folio 150 verso, running header: SUNDAY THEOTOKIA OF THE EIGHT TONES)*
...taking pure flesh from the pure Virgin and coming forth from her with the assumption, is the One Son, twofold in nature, but not in Hypostasis. Therefore, truly proclaiming Him perfect God and perfect man, we confess Christ our God: Whom pray, O unwedded Mother, that He have mercy upon our souls!

*At the Aposticha, Theotokion:*[^807]
O unwedded Virgin, who didst ineffably conceive God in the flesh, O Mother of God the Most High: receive the entreaties of thy servants, O all-immaculate one, granting unto all purification of transgressions: now receiving our supplications, pray that we all may be saved!

*(Tailpiece engraving: Large ornate Baroque cartouche with foliate and floral motifs)*

*(Right page — Folio 151 recto, headpiece engraving: Medallion with IC XC monogram surrounded by cherubim and floral vines)*
*Sunday Dismissal Theotokia after the Troparia of the Saints, of the Eight Tones.*[^808]

*Tone 1. Theotokion:*[^809]
When Gabriel announced unto thee, O Virgin: Rejoice! with the voice the Master of all became incarnate in thee, the holy Ark, as righteous David said: Thou hast appeared more spacious than the heavens, having borne thy Creator: glory to Him Who took up His abode in thee! Glory to Him Who came forth from thee! Glory to Him Who has delivered us through thy birthgiving!

*Tone 2. Theotokion:*[^810]
All thy mysteries are beyond comprehension, all thy mysteries are all-glorious, O Theotokos; for purity being sealed and virginity preserved, thou wast known as a true Mother, having born the true God: pray Him that our souls may be saved!

*Tone 3. Theotokion:*[^811]
We hymn thee who didst mediate the salvation of our race, O Theotokos Virgin; for taking the Passion upon the Cross in the flesh assumed from thee, thy Son and our God has delivered us from corruption, as the Lover of mankind!

*Tone 4. Theotokion:*[^812]
The mystery hidden from the ages and unknown unto angels, was manifested through thee, O Theotokos, unto those upon earth: God becoming incarnate in unconfused union, and voluntarily accepting the Cross for our sake, whereby having raised the first-fashioned man, He saved our souls from death!

*Tone 5. Theotokion:*[^813]
Rejoice, impassable gate of the Lord! Rejoice, rampart and protection of those who hasten unto thee! Rejoice, haven unshaken by storms and unwedded Bride, who didst bear in the flesh thy Maker and God: cease not to pray for those who hymn and worship thy birthgiving!

*Tone 6. Theotokion:*[^814]
Calling Thy Mother blessed, Thou didst come unto the Passion by voluntary will, having shone forth *(Left page — Folio 151 verso, running header: SUNDAY THEOTOKIA OF THE EIGHT TONES)*
...having shone forth upon the Cross, wishing to seek out Adam, saying unto the angels: Rejoice with Me, for the lost coin has been found! O our God Who hast wisely ordered all things, glory to Thee!

*Tone 7. Theotokion:*[^815]
As the treasury of our resurrection, lead up from the pit and the deep of transgressions those who hope in thee, O all-hymned one; for thou didst save those guilty of sin, having born our Salvation, who before birthgiving wast a Virgin, and in birthgiving a Virgin, and after birthgiving again abidest a Virgin!

*Tone 8. Theotokion:*[^816]
He Who for our sake was born of the Virgin, and endured crucifixion, O Good One, Who by death didst overthrow death, and manifested resurrection as God: despise not those whom Thou didst create with Thy hand; show forth Thy love for mankind, O Merciful One; receive the Theotokos who bore Thee, who intercedes for us; and save, O our Savior, a despairing people!

*The End, and unto God be the glory.*[^817]

*(Tailpiece engraving: Ornate Baroque cartouche depicting a winged cherub head enveloped in luxuriant floral and acanthus leaf scrollwork)*

*(Right page — Rear flyleaf / endpaper)*
*(Flyleaf — Archival binding sheet: blank verso with cursive Cyrillic ink ownership notations and rectangular purple ink accession stamp of the State Museum of Books and Printing of Ukraine, Fund No. 30552, GK No. 443) (Rear cover — 1785 Kyiv-Pechersk Lavra dark brown marbled calfskin binding, with blind-tooled triple fillet perimeter frames, vertical leather spine-creases, and rubbed antique shelf patina)*

# FOOTNOTES AND SCHOLARLY COMMENTARY



[^1]: *Poluchnik* (получникъ): 19th-century Ukrainian/Ruthenian administrative term denoting a pensioner or recipient of an allowance, here identifying the codex's private owner, Yevstafiy Savych, who recorded his ownership as a parental blessing on February 17, 1836.

[^2]: Catherine II ('the Great'), Empress of Russia (reigned 1762–1796). The imperial family members enumerated are the Grand Duke Paul Petrovich (later Emperor Paul I), his wife Maria Feodorovna (Sophie Dorothea of Württemberg), and their children Alexander (later Emperor Alexander I), Constantine, Alexandra, and Elena.

[^3]: Archimandrite Zosima (Valkevich), Archimandrite of the Kyiv-Pechersk Lavra from 1762 to 1786. Under his tenure, the Lavra printing press issued numerous landmark liturgical editions.

[^4]: Engraver monogram (СФНМ) in Cyrillic, identifying the master engraver of the Kyiv-Pechersk Lavra workshop who executed the Dormition copperplate/woodcut.

[^5]: The feast of the Synaxis of the Venerable Fathers of the Kyiv Caves who rest in the Near Caves (St. Anthony's Caves) is celebrated on the first Saturday following the Apodosis of the Elevation of the Holy Cross (September 21), which places it between September 22 and September 28 in the Julian calendar.

[^6]: *The bell-ringer strikes the klepalo* (Клиентъ звонитъ): The physical rubric uses *klient* (a Lavra monastic colloquialism/corruption of *klepalo*, semantron or bell-ringer) directing the ringer to sound the klepalo/bells for Great Vespers. The 20th Kathisma antiphon refers to the final kathisma of the liturgical Psalter (Psalms 134–142, "O give thanks unto the Lord"), prescribed for Friday evening Vespers.

[^7]: *Ruthenian land* (рѡссійская земле): The historical Kyivan recension applies this term to the lands of Kyivan Rus' and Ukraine, here celebrated as the spiritual cradle of the Caves monastics.

[^8]: The rubric directs the choir to the Dismissal Theotokia (*Otpostitelny*) printed at the end of the codex on folio 151 (physical leaf p155).

[^9]: The three festal Paremias for venerable monastics are taken from the Septuagint text of the Wisdom of Solomon: Wisdom 3:1–9; Wisdom 5:15–6:3; Wisdom 4:7–15.

[^10]: The melody model *Tone 3, Podoben: "Thou Art the Trinity"* (Троица еси) governs the Sessional Hymn after the first Kathisma.

[^11]: The Gospel reading for monastic hierarchs and ascetics: Matthew 4:23–5:1 (Slavic Pericope 10), recounting Christ teaching and healing throughout Galilee, followed by the ascent unto the mountain before the Sermon on the Mount.

[^12]: Saint Barlaam, first Abbot of the Kyiv Caves Monastery (commemorated November 19), who left boyar rank and wealth to accept the monastic tonsure under Saint Anthony.

[^13]: Saint Isaiah of the Caves, later Bishop of Rostov (commemorated May 15, d. 1090), and Saint Simon, Bishop of Vladimir and Suzdal (commemorated May 10, d. 1226), one of the principal epistolary compilers of the Kyiv Caves Paterikon.

[^14]: Saint Ilya of Murom (St. Elijah of Murom, commemorated December 19, d. c. 1188), the epic warrior of Kyivan Rus' who took monastic vows in the Kyiv Caves Monastery; his incorrupt relics in the Near Caves bear an unhealed spear wound in the left hand and chest.

[^15]: Saint Nikon the Dry (commemorated December 11, early 12th century), captured by the Polovtsians and held captive for three years under severe torment, refusing ransom and bearing torture with extraordinary endurance until he was miraculously delivered.

[^16]: Saint Prokhor the "Orache-Eater" (*Lebednik*, commemorated February 10, d. 1107), who gathered wild orache (*chenopodium*) to bake bread for the starving populace during the severe Kyiv famine and miraculous transformed wood ashes into pure white salt.

[^17]: Saint Mercurius, Bishop of Smolensk (commemorated August 7, d. 1239), whose body according to the Kyiv Caves tradition miraculously floated up the Dnieper River in a wooden coffin to the Lavra, where his relics were laid to rest in the Near Caves.

[^18]: Saint Nestor the Chronicler (commemorated October 27, c. 1056–1114), monk of the Kyiv Caves and renowned chronicler, author of the *Primary Chronicle* (Tale of Bygone Years) and the hagiographical lives of Saints Boris and Gleb and Saint Theodosius of the Caves.

[^19]: Hieromartyr Eustratius of the Caves (commemorated March 28, d. 1096), captured during a Polovtsian raid on Kyiv, sold into slavery in Chersonese (Crimea), and crucified on Pascha for refusing to renounce Christ, compared in the hymn unto the Prophet Elijah ascending to heaven in a fiery chariot.

[^20]: Saint Matthew the Clairvoyant (commemorated October 5), monk of the Kyiv Caves, endowed with the prophetic gift of seeing demonic temptations and hidden thoughts.

[^21]: Saint Ephraim the Eunuch, Bishop of Pereyaslav (commemorated January 28), who traveled to Constantinople and Mount Athos and brought back the Studite Typikon for Saint Theodosius.

[^22]: Saint Mark the Grave-Digger (commemorated December 29), who dug graves in the Near Caves for thirty years and at whose command corpses obeyed his voice.

[^23]: Saint Gregory the Wonderworker of the Caves (commemorated January 8, d. 1093), drowned in the Dnieper River by order of Prince Rostislav Vsevolodovich after prophesying the prince's defeat and death.

[^24]: Saint Erasmus (commemorated February 24), who gave his wealth to adorn the Lavra Dormition Church with icons, and Saint Onesimus the Recluse (commemorated October 4).

[^25]: Saint Spyridon and Saint Nicodemus the Prosphora-Bakers (commemorated October 31), who for thirty years baked prosphora while continually chanting the Psalter.

[^26]: Saint Alipius the Iconographer (commemorated August 17) and Saint Gregory the Iconographer, who learned icon painting from the Byzantine masters who decorated the Great Lavra Church.

[^27]: Saint Pimen the Faster (commemorated May 8) and Saint Ephraim the Priest.

[^28]: Saint Onuphrius the Silent (commemorated July 21), Saint Isaiah the Recluse, and Saint Sylvester the Chronicler (commemorated January 2).

[^29]: The foursome of princely ascetics: Saint Vladimir the Great, Equal-to-the-Apostles (commemorated July 15); the passion-bearers Boris and Gleb (baptized Roman and David, commemorated May 2 and July 24); and Prince Sviatoslav of Chernihiv, tonsured as Monk Nikola Sviatosha (commemorated October 14).

[^30]: Saint Barbara the Great-Martyr (commemorated December 4), whose relics were brought to Kyiv in 1108; Saint Euphrosyne, Princess of Polotsk (commemorated May 23, d. 1173 in Jerusalem, whose incorrupt body was kept in the Kyiv Caves until 1910); and Righteous Juliana, Princess of Olshansk (commemorated July 6, c. 1540).

[^31]: The assembly of the Hierarchs of Rus' enumerated in the Exapostilarion includes the Metropolitans of Kyiv and Moscow: Michael I, Hilarion, Peter, Alexis, Jonah, Photius, and Cyprian; Bishops Ephraim of Pereyaslav, Nicholas of Tmutarakan, and Nikita of Novgorod; Macarius, Metropolitan of Kyiv killed by the Tatars in 1497; Leontius, Bishop of Rostov; and Simon, Bishop of Vladimir and Suzdal.

[^32]: Epistle reading for the feast: 2 Corinthians 11:21–12:9 (Pericope 176), recounting the Apostle Paul's ascetic labors, persecutions, visions, and the thorn in the flesh.

[^33]: Gospel reading for monastics: Matthew 11:27–30 (Pericope 43), the invitation of Christ: "Come unto Me, all ye that labor and are heavy laden, and I will give you rest."

[^34]: Saint Michael I, traditionally revered as the first Metropolitan of Kyiv (reigned c. 988–992), sent from Constantinople to baptize Prince Vladimir and the people of Rus'.

[^35]: *Tsargrad* (Ца́рьгра́дъ): Constantinople, imperial capital of the Byzantine Empire and source of the baptism and ecclesiastical hierarchy of Kyivan Rus'.

[^36]: The prophecy of the Apostle Andrew the First-Called, who according to the *Primary Chronicle* stood upon the hills of Kyiv, planted a cross, and prophesied that great churches would arise there and grace would shine forth upon the land.

[^37]: The rubric prescribes the common festal Paremias for a holy hierarch from Proverbs 10, Proverbs 10–11, and Wisdom 4, located in the general festal apparatus at folio 151.

[^38]: A dramatic prosopopoeia depicting the lament and terror of the pagan demonic idols when Saint Michael arrived in Kyiv, cast down the statues of Perun, and established the Christian faith.

[^39]: Fulfilling the aforementioned prophecy of Saint Andrew the First-Called upon the Kyivan hills.

[^40]: Gospel reading at Matins: John 10:9–16 (Pericope 36), the Good Shepherd pericope: "I am the door: by Me if any man enter in, he shall be saved..."

[^41]: Typological parallel to the fall of the walls of Jericho before the Ark of the Covenant under Joshua (Joshua 6), prefiguring the collapse of Kyivan idols before the Cross and Saint Michael.

[^42]: The gold neck-chain (*hryvnia*, гривна) worn by nobility in Kyivan Rus', here employed as a metaphor for the holy Gospel adorning the neck of the baptized city of Kyiv.

[^43]: Saint Michael is likened to a second Moses leading the new Israel out of Egyptian idolatry, citing the prophetic verse of Psalm 71:16 (LXX 71:16): "There shall be an establishment of faith in the earth, upon the top of the mountains."

[^44]: The destruction of the idol of Perun upon the hills of Kyiv in 988, as recorded in the *Primary Chronicle* (Tale of Bygone Years): the wooden statue with a silver head and gold mustache was tied to a horse's tail, dragged down the Borychiv descent, beaten with rods, and cast into the Dnieper River.

[^45]: The relics of Saint Michael originally rested in the Church of the Tithes (*Desiatynna*) built by Prince Vladimir, and were later translated to the Kyiv Caves Lavra Dormition Cathedral, where they rested incorrupt.

[^46]: The feast of Saint Michael on September 30 falls at the conclusion of September, the first month of the Byzantine liturgical year (Indiction), hence celebrated as the "first rotation of the new year."

[^47]: Prince Vladimir the Great is here designated by his Christian baptismal name Basil (*Vasiliy*), after his imperial sponsor Emperor Basil II of Byzantium.

[^48]: Woodcut engraving of Saint Damian the Presbyter and Healer (commemorated October 5, d. 1071), first physician and healer of the Kyiv Caves Monastery, who ministered to the sick with prayer and sanctified oil. In the lower register, Saint Theodosius appears to him on his deathbed in an angelic vision promising him the Kingdom of Heaven.

[^49]: Saint Jeremiah the Clairvoyant (commemorated October 5, c. 1070), who was tonsured under Saint Anthony and received the gift of discerning the secret thoughts and spiritual struggles of the brethren.

[^50]: Saint Matthew the Clairvoyant (commemorated October 5, late 11th century), who saw demonic spirits in the form of dark "Ethiopians" (a traditional monastic idiom for demons in early Christian and Kyivan literature) attempting to seduce the monastics.

[^51]: The vision of Saint Matthew recorded in the *Kyiv Caves Paterikon* (Discourse 20): while standing during the service, he saw a demon in the guise of a soldier walking through the church and throwing sticky flowers (*lyepki*) onto the monks' robes; those to whom the flower stuck became drowsy and left the service before the dismissal, whereas those who resisted were untouched.

[^52]: Saint Theodosius of the Caves (commemorated May 3 and August 14), Abbot and co-founder of the Kyiv Caves Monastery, spiritual father of Saint Damian, whose appearance the angel assumed when visiting Damian on his sickbed.

[^53]: The church semantron (*bilo* / *klepalo*, би́ло): a resonant wooden board suspended outside the monastery church, struck with a wooden mallet to summon the brethren to services. Saint Matthew was resting beneath it after Matins when he saw a legion of demons marching toward a monk's cell.

[^54]: The Great Lavra Church of the Dormition of the Most Holy Theotokos, miraculously founded in 1073 and consecrated in 1089, regarded as the earthly dwelling of the Mother of God, who sent Greek master-builders from Constantinople with her icon and relics.

[^55]: Prince Sviatoslav Davydovych (monastic name Nikola Sviatosha, c. 1080–1143), Prince of Lutsk and Chernihiv, son of Davyd Sviatoslavych and grandson of Grand Prince Sviatoslav II of Kyiv. In 1106 he renounced his princely rank and entered the Kyiv-Pechersk Monastery, becoming the first East Slavic prince to embrace monasticism. He served as gatekeeper, kitchen laborer, and orchardist, and funded the construction of the Holy Trinity Gate Church (Troitska Nadbramna tserkva) above the monastery gates.

[^56]: The three Old Testament readings for a Venerable Father (Wisdom 3:1–9; Wisdom 5:15–6:3; Wisdom 4:7–15) are indexed to folio 30 verso of the 1785 print (physical leaf p44), where common festal readings are printed in full.

[^57]: Slavonic typographical error in the 1785 Lavra print: *па́пене* (papene) appears on Folio 18 recto as an epigraphic misprint for *прпⷠне* (prepodobne, "O venerable").

[^58]: Saint Joasaph of India: The protagonist of the Christianized tale of Barlaam and Josaphat (*Повѣсть о Варлаамѣ и Іоасафѣ*), traditionally attributed to Saint John Damascene, who renounced his royal inheritance to pursue asceticism in the desert, serving as the quintessential patristic exemplar for Christian royalty renouncing secular rule.

[^59]: Prince Nikola Sviatosha served for three continuous years in the monastery kitchen (*пова́рня*) chopping wood and preparing meals for the brethren; when his brothers Princes Iziaslav and Vladimir urged him to cease such menial labor, he requested permission to remain for an additional year.

[^60]: Saint Nikola Sviatosha served for three continuous years as the monastery gatekeeper, sitting by the outer gates of the Kyiv-Pechersk Lavra, strictly regulating entry and greeting all visitors with deep prostrations, regardless of their social rank.

[^61]: Following his three years as gatekeeper, Prince Nikola was assigned to the monastery refectory (*трапеза*), where he washed dishes, carried water, and set out food for the monks.

[^62]: At the end of his cycle of menial obediences, the superior and brethren directed Saint Nikola to work in the monastery garden and orchard (*вертоград*), where he tended trees and produced fruits for the community.

[^63]: The Syrian or Greek physician Peter, who had served as court physician to Prince Nikola before his tonsure, was sent by Nikola's brother Prince Iziaslav to admonish him to stop degrading himself through menial labor and harsh fasting; Saint Nikola instead converted Peter to monastic life.

[^64]: Saint Nikola Sviatosha had excavated a burial niche for himself in the Near Caves of Saint Anthony; when the former physician Peter fell mortally ill and wept that he had not prepared a tomb, Saint Nikola ceded his own prepared burial place to Peter.

[^65]: Codicological and hagiographical witness from the *Kyiv-Pechersk Paterikon*: Following the tonsure and death of the monk Peter, Saint Nikola Sviatosha lived an additional thirty years in the monastery without leaving its walls, falling asleep in the Lord around 1143.

[^66]: The contact relics of Saint Nikola Sviatosha: his wooden headrest, brass pectoral cross, and hairshirt (*власяница*). According to the Paterikon, during an illness of Prince Iziaslav, Saint Nikola sent him his hairshirt, which immediately restored the prince to health; Iziaslav later wore it into battle as an invulnerable armor.

[^67]: Saint Nestor the Chronicler (c. 1056–1114), monk of the Kyiv-Pechersk Monastery, disciple of Saint Theodosius, and author of the fundamental Ruthenian chronicle *The Tale of Bygone Years* (*Повѣсть временныхъ лѣтъ* / *Primary Chronicle*), as well as the *Life of Saint Theodosius* and the *Passion of Saints Boris and Gleb*.

[^68]: Saint Nestor entered the Kyiv-Pechersk Lavra at the age of seventeen during the abbacy of Saint Theodosius, under whose spiritual direction he was trained in monastic ascetical discipline, later receiving the diaconate from Abbot Stephen.

[^69]: Reference to the miraculous foundation of the Great Lavra Church of the Dormition (1073): according to the *Paterikon*, dew fell upon the entire region except the foundation site, and fire fell from heaven to consume the dew and trace the foundation boundaries, an event witnessed by Saint Nestor as a young novice.

[^70]: Saint Nestor was ordained to the holy diaconate by Abbot Stephen (successor of Saint Theodosius), as recorded in his *Life* in the *Kyiv-Pechersk Paterikon*.

[^71]: In August 1091, under Abbot John, Saint Nestor was appointed to excavate and uncover the incorrupt relics of Saint Theodosius from the Far Caves, subsequently participating in their solemn translation into the Great Dormition Church on August 14.

[^72]: Saint Barlaam, first Abbot of the Kyiv-Pechersk Monastery (c. 1020–1065), son of the Kyivan boyar Ivan Yanovych; tonsured by Saint Anthony despite the fierce opposition of Grand Prince Iziaslav and his father; later appointed by Iziaslav as superior of the Monastery of Saint Demetrius in Kyiv. While returning from a pilgrimage to Constantinople and Mount Athos, he fell asleep in the Lord at the Holy Mountain Monastery in Volodymyr-Volynskyi; his relics were later translated to the Near Caves of Saint Anthony.

[^73]: Psalm 111:1 (LXX).

[^74]: Psalm 115:6 (LXX).

[^75]: Reference to Saint Anthony of the Caves, under whose spiritual direction Saint Barlaam placed himself upon entering the monastic life.

[^76]: Cf. Psalm 41:2 (LXX): "As the hart pants after the fountains of water..."

[^77]: Reference to Saints Anthony and Theodosius of the Caves living in the subterranean cave; cf. Matthew 5:15.

[^78]: Cf. Philippians 3:8.

[^79]: Saint Barlaam was the son of the prominent Kyivan boyar Ivan Yanovych; prior to his tonsure, his parents had betrothed him to a noble maiden, whom he renounced to embrace asceticism in the cave.

[^80]: Diplomatic Imperial titulature in the 1785 Kyiv-Pechersk Lavra edition, referring to Empress Catherine II (reigned 1762–1796).

[^81]: Folio 30 verso of the 1785 print corresponds to physical leaf p35, where the three common Old Testament readings for a Venerable Father (Wisdom 3:1–9; Wisdom 5:15–6:3; Wisdom 4:7–15) are indexed or printed.

[^82]: Saint Anthony of the Caves (c. 983–1073), founder of Kyivan monasticism, who tonsured Saint Barlaam and appointed him the first abbot of the Kyiv-Pechersk Monastery.

[^83]: Cf. Psalm 44:3 (LXX): "Thou art fairer than the sons of men..."

[^84]: Psalm 115:6 (LXX).

[^85]: Psalm 91:13 (LXX).

[^86]: Paraphrase of Psalm 138:17 (LXX): "Exceedingly precious unto me are Thy friends, O God; exceedingly strengthened have their principalities become."

[^88]: Psalm 67:36 (LXX).

[^89]: Psalm 67:27 (LXX).

[^90]: Psalm 115:6 (LXX).

[^91]: Diplomatic Imperial titulature in the 1785 Lavra print, invoking divine protection for Empress Catherine II.

[^92]: Cf. Philippians 3:13: "Forgetting those things which are behind, and reaching forth unto those things which are before..."

[^93]: Cf. Matthew 13:44.

[^94]: Cf. Psalm 117:22 (LXX); Matthew 21:42; 1 Peter 2:6–7.

[^95]: Psalm 50:12 (LXX).

[^96]: Habakkuk 3:2–3 (LXX).

[^97]: The monastic tonsure and investiture of Saint Barlaam by Saint Anthony of the Caves: as recorded in Discourse 11 of the *Kyiv-Pechersk Paterikon*, Barlaam's father, the Kyivan boyar Ivan Yanovych, attacked the cave with an armed retinue, forcibly dragged Barlaam out, tore off his monastic mantle and cast it into a ravine, stripped away his klobuk ("helmet of salvation"), and forced him into rich boyar robes.

[^98]: *Kyiv-Pechersk Paterikon*, Discourse 11: Upon being forcibly dressed in boyar attire, Saint Barlaam threw the garments into the mud and trampled them underfoot; when dragged to his father's estate, he refused to participate in the banquet, sat in a solitary corner, and maintained three days of silent fasting and prayer.

[^99]: Psalm 149:5 (LXX).

[^100]: Jonah 2:1–11 (LXX).

[^101]: Psalm 33:18 (LXX).

[^102]: *Kyiv-Pechersk Paterikon*, Discourse 11: Following three days of unwavering ascetic resistance and the collective prayers of the cave community, Saint Barlaam's father relented and allowed him to return in peace to Saint Anthony in the Caves.

[^103]: The Three Holy Children in the Babylonian furnace (Daniel 3:26–56).

[^104]: Around 1058, as the monastic brotherhood in the Caves grew to twelve ascetics, Saint Anthony appointed Saint Barlaam as the first Abbot (Ihegumen) of the Kyiv-Pechersk Monastery and withdrew to a neighboring hill to dig a new solitary cave (now the Near Caves).

[^105]: *Kyiv-Pechersk Paterikon*, Discourse 11: Saint Barlaam undertook a pilgrimage to Jerusalem and Mount Athos to visit the holy sites and monastic centers of the Christian East, receiving the blessing of the elders before returning to Kyiv.

[^106]: Following a second pilgrimage to Constantinople around 1065 to procure sacred icons and church vessels, Saint Barlaam fell mortally ill on his return journey and passed away at the Holy Mountain Monastery (*Святогорский монастырь*) in Volodymyr-Volynskyi, directing his companions to convey his body back to the Kyiv-Pechersk Lavra for burial in the Caves.

[^107]: The Slavonic text labels this prosomoion as Tone 3 (*Гласъ, г҃. Подо́бенъ: Да́лъ є҆сѝ зна́менїе*); the canonical model *Да́лъ є҆сѝ зна́менїе* ("Thou hast given a sign") is customarily set in Tone 4 in the Ruthenian and Byzantine hymnographic tradition.

[^108]: Reference to Saint Anthony of the Caves.

[^109]: Saint Athanasius the Recluse of the Caves (c. 1176), an ascetic of the Near Caves whose life is recorded in Discourse 18 of the *Kyiv-Pechersk Paterikon*. Following a severe illness, he died and lay unburied for two days while the brethren prepared his grave; on the third day, the monks found him sitting up alive, weeping bitterly. He subsequently walled himself inside a dark cave cell for twelve years, speaking only to urge total repentance, strict obedience, and ceaseless prayer, weeping continuously until his peaceful falling asleep.

[^110]: John 11:25–26.

[^111]: Cf. Psalm 15:10 (LXX): "Neither wilt Thou suffer Thine holy one to see corruption."

[^112]: Matthew 5:4: "Blessed are they that mourn: for they shall be comforted."

[^113]: Habakkuk 3:11.

[^114]: *Kyiv-Pechersk Paterikon*, Discourse 18: When the astonished brethren implored the resurrected monk to tell them what he had seen in the realm of the dead, Saint Athanasius replied only: "Keep obedience unto the superior in all things; repent unceasingly; and pray unto the Lord Jesus Christ, His most pure Mother, and the venerable Anthony and Theodosius, that you may finish your life here; for great is the benefit of obedience."

[^115]: Reference to the patristic teaching on the aerial toll-houses (*мытарства*), through which the departing soul passes, defended by the fiery wings of prayer and unceasing repentance.

[^116]: *Kyiv-Pechersk Paterikon*, Discourse 18: After speaking his three counsels on obedience, repentance, and prayer, Saint Athanasius walled up the entrance to his cave cell, leaving only a small aperture through which a little water and bread were passed on alternate days, remaining in total seclusion for twelve years without lighting a candle.

[^117]: Etymological wordplay on the name *Athanasios* (Greek: Ἀθανάσιος, from ἀ- "not" + θάνατος "death" = "immortal" / "deathless"): "As one named for immortality (*безсмертия тезоименит*), after thy death thou didst rise alive."

[^118]: *Kyiv-Pechersk Paterikon*, Discourse 18: Following the falling asleep of Saint Athanasius, a monk named Babylas (Vavila), who had been blind and paralyzed in his legs for many years, was carried to the cell of the recluse, wept over his body, and embraced his feet, instantly receiving complete sight and the ability to walk.

[^119]: Luke 1:51–53, 78–79 (LXX Canticle of Zechariah / Magnificat).

[^120]: Melodic model: Tone 2, *Подо́бенъ: Є҆гда̀ ѿ дре́ва* ("When from the Tree").

[^121]: Typographical error in the 1785 Kyiv-Pechersk Lavra edition: *По́пне* (popne) appears on Folio 36 recto as an epigraphic misprint for *Прпⷠне* (prepodobne, "O venerable one").

[^122]: St. Nikon the Dry (*Никонъ Сухїй*, d. ca. 1101) was a monk of the Kyiv Caves Monastery. In 1096, during the raid on the Lavra led by the Polovtsian Khan Boniak, Nikon was taken captive along with St. Eustratius of the Caves and other monks. Nikon remained a captive in the Polovtsian steppe for over three years, enduring cruel tortures, starvation, and exposure to burning heat and freezing cold. Because his body became completely emaciated and dried from these tortures and fasts, he received the cognomen "the Dry."

[^123]: According to the *Kyiv Caves Paterikon* (Discourse 24), after three years of captivity, as Nikon predicted his impending release, his Polovtsian master cut the tendons of his legs to prevent escape. A few days later, Nikon was suddenly and invisibly transported through the air to the Dormition Cathedral of the Caves Lavra while the brethren were chanting the Eucharistic Canon during the Divine Liturgy.

[^124]: Referring to the Polovtsians (Cumans), a nomadic Turkic people of the Pontic-Caspian steppe whose recurring incursions against Kyivan Rus' in the 11th–13th centuries included catastrophic raids on the Kyiv-Pechersk Lavra.

[^125]: An allusion to the Song of Moses (Exod. 15:1–21 [LXX]).

[^126]: Cf. 1 Kingdoms (1 Sam.) 2:5 [LXX]: "For the barren hath borne seven; and she that had many children is waxed feeble."

[^127]: Slavonic *мета́нїи* (from Greek μετάνοιαι), prostrations (metanias) performed as part of the monastic rule of prayer and ascetic labor.

[^128]: Cf. Psalm 125:5–6 [LXX]: "They that sow with tears shall reap with joy. They went on and wept, casting their seeds; but they shall surely come with rejoicing, bringing their sheaves."

[^129]: In the *Paterikon*, Nikon's relatives repeatedly attempted to send gold and silver to ransom him from the Polovtsians, but Nikon strictly forbade them, choosing rather to suffer voluntary affliction for Christ's sake.

[^130]: Psalm 93:12 [LXX].

[^131]: Cf. Psalm 48:13, 21 [LXX]: "Man being in honor understood not: he was compared to the senseless beasts, and became like unto them."

[^132]: Psalm 68:2 [LXX].

[^133]: Psalm 67:7 [LXX]: "God settles the solitary in a house; bringing out those who are bound with courage."

[^134]: Slavonic *подрѣ́занїя ли́ствецъ* (cutting of the shins/calves), referring to the severing of the Achilles tendons and hamstrings by the Polovtsian captor to permanently lame Nikon so he could not flee.

[^135]: Psalm 69:2 [LXX]: "O God, attend unto my help; make haste to help me, O Lord."

[^136]: Psalm 30:2 [LXX]: "In Thee, O Lord, have I hoped, let me never be ashamed; deliver me in Thy righteousness."

[^137]: Referring to the Prophet Habakkuk, who was carried through the air by an angel by the hair of his head from Judea to Babylon to bring food to Daniel in the lions' den (Bel and the Dragon / Dan. 14:33–39 [LXX]).

[^138]: St. Stephen the Protomartyr (Acts 7:59–60), who prayed for his persecutors as he was being stoned. St. Nikon likewise prayed for his Polovtsian master, who after Nikon's miraculous translation came to the Lavra, recognized him, converted to Christ, was baptized, and tonsured a monk under Nikon's spiritual direction.

[^139]: Cf. Daniel 3 [LXX] (The Prayer of Azariah and the Song of the Three Holy Children).

[^140]: James 5:16: "The effectual fervent prayer of a righteous man avails much."

[^141]: Cf. 2 Corinthians 4:7: "But we have this treasure in earthen vessels, that the excellency of the power may be of God, and not of us."

[^142]: St. Mark the Gravedigger (*Марко гробокопатель*, 11th–12th c.) and St. Theophilus the Weeper (*Ѳеофилъ слезоточивый*, 11th–12th c.) are commemorated together on December 24. Mark dug graves for the brethren of the Kyiv Caves Lavra, and was granted power over the dead; Theophilus was his disciple who wept for many years in deep repentance.

[^143]: An ascetical patristic metaphor for the flesh and carnal passions as an "internal/domestic enemy" (*домашній врагъ*).

[^144]: The liturgical texts for December 24 incorporate the Stichera and Troparia of the Forefeast of the Nativity of Christ, celebrated on the same day in the Byzantine Menaion.

[^145]: Cross-reference to the three Old Testament readings common to a Venerable Father (fol. 30v = leaf p35).

[^146]: An event from Discourse 33 of the *Kyiv Caves Paterikon*: a monk died, and when Mark went to anoint him with oil, there was no space to reach him; Mark commanded the deceased monk to pour the oil upon himself, and the dead man sat up, took the vessel, poured the oil over his head and breast, gave back the vessel, and lay down again.

[^147]: Another miracle recorded in Discourse 33: a brother died before Mark could finish digging his grave, so Mark commanded the dead brother to wait until morning; the dead man opened his eyes and remained alive until the grave was ready the next day, at which point he peacefully fell asleep again.

[^148]: Cf. Matthew 25:14–30 (The Parable of the Talents) and Matthew 20:1–16 (The Parable of the Laborers in the Vineyard).

[^149]: Luke 10:19–21 (Pericope 44), read at Matins for monastic and ascetic saints.

[^150]: Slavonic *ѡ҆ кро́та бо ме́да на то́кмо пи́лъ еси́ во́ду* - printed with explicit typographic brackets in the 1785 Kyiv-Pechersk edition, highlighting St. Mark's total abstinence from mead and wine in favor of water.

[^151]: Habakkuk 3:2–3 [LXX].

[^152]: John 14:12: "Truly, truly, I say unto you, he that believes in Me, the works that I do shall he do also; and greater works than these shall he do; because I go unto My Father."

[^153]: An allusion to John 11:38–44, comparing the cave-sepulcher of the Caves with the garden-tomb of Lazarus.

[^154]: Ephesians 2:14: "For He is our peace, Who hath made both one, and hath broken down the middle wall of partition between us."

[^155]: Romans 8:28: "And we know that all things work together for good to them that love God, to them who are the called according to His purpose."

[^156]: Exodus 3:2 [LXX] (The Burning Bush).

[^157]: Jonah 2:1–10.

[^158]: The miracle of the two brothers, John and Theophilus, recorded in Discourse 33 of the *Kyiv Caves Paterikon*. When John died first, Mark buried him in the higher sepulcher. When Theophilus complained that the senior place belonged to him, Mark commanded the dead John to yield the place, and the deceased brother rose up and moved to the lower place. Terrified by this miracle, Theophilus spent the rest of his life in unbroken weeping and repentance.

[^159]: Discourse 33 of the *Kyiv Caves Paterikon* recounts that Theophilus wept so copiously throughout his remaining years that he filled whole vessels with his tears. An angel subsequently appeared to him, showing him a flask containing his tears and announcing that God had accepted his repentance.

[^160]: Matthew 13:45–46 (The Parable of the Pearl of Great Price).

[^161]: Slavonic *въ двои́хъ хотя́щихъ и дѣ́йствїихъ сꙋ́щему* (literally, "Who existest in two willings and operations"): a direct liturgical affirmation of the Sixth Ecumenical Council (Constantinople III, 680–681), which dogmatized that Jesus Christ possesses two natural wills and two operations (divine and human) corresponding to His two natures.

[^162]: Luke 1:78–79 (The Canticle of Zechariah).

[^163]: The canonical Nativity Forefeast Sticheron at the Praises for December 24.

[^164]: St. Gregory the Wonderworker of the Caves (*Григорій чюдотворецъ*, d. 1093) is commemorated on January 8. In 1093, when Prince Rostislav Vsevolodovich of Pereyaslav was riding to join his brother Vladimir Monomakh in battle against the Polovtsians, his soldiers mocked Gregory near the Dnieper. The saint admonished them to repent, prophesying that Rostislav and his soldiers would drown in water. Enraged, the prince commanded that Gregory be bound with a heavy millstone around his neck and drowned in the Dnieper. Later that year, after being defeated by the Polovtsians at the Battle of the Stuhna River, Prince Rostislav fled on horseback across the river and drowned, exactly as Gregory had prophesied (*Kyiv Caves Paterikon*, Discourse 23).

[^165]: Cf. Psalm 131:4 (LXX): "If I give sleep unto mine eyes, or slumber unto mine eyelids, or rest unto my temples..."

[^166]: Rubrical reference directing the reader to the common readings for venerable monastic fathers provided on Folio 1 verso of the codex (leaves p6–p7).

[^167]: St. Gregory the Wonderworker of the Caves (†1093, commemorated January 8), disciple of St. Theodosius of the Caves, renowned for his clairvoyance, prayer against demons, and martyr's death by drowning at the hands of Prince Rostislav Vsevolodovich of Pereyaslav.

[^168]: Psalm 65:12 (LXX): "We went through fire and water, and Thou didst bring us out into refreshment."

[^169]: Luke pericope 24 (composite liturgical reading from Luke 6:17–23), the standard festal Gospel lection for Venerable Fathers at Matins.

[^170]: Heirmos of Ode 1 of the Canon for the Feast of the Theophany of the Lord, Tone 2, composed by St. Cosmas the Melodist (Bishop of Maiuma, c. 706–760).

[^171]: Heirmos of Ode 3 of the Canon for Theophany, Tone 2 (alluding to 1 Samuel / 1 Kings 2:10).

[^172]: According to the *Paterikon of the Kyiv Caves* (Discourse 24), three separate bands of thieves came to rob St. Gregory's cell and garden; the first sought his manuscripts, but were struck with supernatural sleep for five days and nights until the saint awoke them.

[^173]: Heirmos of Ode 4 of the Canon for Theophany, Tone 2 (alluding to Habakkuk 3:1–3 and 1 Corinthians 1:24).

[^174]: Cf. Proverbs 25:21 and Romans 12:20: "If thine enemy hunger, feed him; if he thirst, give him drink."

[^175]: The municipal judge seized the thieves and prepared to torture them according to civil law; St. Gregory gave his own valued Sluzhebnik to the magistrate to secure their unconditional pardon and release.

[^176]: Heirmos of Ode 5 of the Canon for Theophany, Tone 2 (alluding to Ephesians 2:14–16 and Philippians 4:7).

[^177]: Discourse 24 of the *Paterikon*: the second band of thieves broke into St. Gregory's apple orchard, but their bodies were immobilized under the weight of the stolen sacks for two days and two nights.

[^178]: Heirmos of Ode 6 of the Canon for Theophany, Tone 2 (alluding to Jonah 2:1–10 and Isaiah 40:3).

[^179]: Discourse 24 of the *Paterikon*: the third incident involved three deceitful men who feigned poverty and asked for clothing, claiming one of them had died; St. Gregory gave the garments while prophesying that the conspirator who feigned death would soon die by hanging, which occurred that same day.

[^180]: Kontakion commemorating St. Gregory's martyrdom in 1093, when he was cast into the Dnieper River with a heavy stone tied to his neck by Prince Rostislav Vsevolodovich.

[^181]: Heirmos of Ode 7 of the Canon for Theophany, Tone 2 (alluding to the Song of the Three Holy Children in Daniel 3).

[^182]: According to the *Paterikon of the Kyiv Caves* (Discourse 24), when St. Gregory asked the conspirators by what death the supposed deceased man would die, they scoffingly replied "he shall be hanged on a tree," which was fulfilled that day when the man was found strangled on an apple branch.

[^183]: Heirmos of Ode 8 of the Canon for Theophany, Tone 2 (alluding to Daniel 3:49–50).

[^184]: Discourse 24 of the *Paterikon*: the thief who climbed the apple tree in St. Gregory's orchard slipped, and his clothing caught on a broken branch, hanging him.

[^185]: Heirmos of Ode 9 of the Canon for Theophany, Tone 2 (composed by St. Cosmas the Melodist).

[^186]: While drawing water at the Dnieper River, St. Gregory was mocked by the young warriors of Prince Rostislav Vsevolodovich; the saint foretold that both they and their prince would drown during their coming battle against the Polovtsians.

[^187]: Proverbs 9:8: "Reprove not a scorner, lest he hate thee: rebuke a wise man, and he will love thee."

[^188]: Enraged by the prophecy of his doom, Prince Rostislav ordered St. Gregory bound and cast into the Dnieper; soon after, on May 26, 1093, Rostislav's army was defeated at the Battle of the Stuhna River, and the prince drowned while attempting to cross the swollen river.

[^189]: The Kyiv Caves *Paterikon* relates that three days after his drowning, St. Gregory's body was found in his locked cell, miraculously transported and dried of river weeds, with the heavy stone still attached to his neck.

[^190]: St. Ephraim, Bishop of Pereyaslav (†ca. 1098, commemorated January 28), former boyar and treasurer (*eunuch*) to Grand Prince Izyaslav Yaroslavich of Kyiv, tonsured by St. Antony of the Caves, later visited Constantinople where he transcribed the Studite Rule for the Pechersk Lavra, and built cathedral churches, public baths, and free hospitals in Pereyaslav.

[^191]: Composite Old Testament paroemia drawn from Proverbs 10:7, 3:13–16, 8:1–4, appointed for feasts of venerable and hierarchical fathers.

[^192]: Composite Old Testament paroemia from the Wisdom of Solomon (Wisdom 7:30, 8:2–4, 8:7–9, 9:1–14).

[^193]: Composite Old Testament paroemia from the Wisdom of Solomon (Wisdom 4:7–14, 2:12–24) appointed for venerable and holy hierarchs.

[^194]: At the request of St. Theodosius of the Kyiv Caves, St. Ephraim traveled to Constantinople around 1068–1070 and transcribed the complete liturgical and monastic Typikon (*Ustav*) of the Studite Monastery, which was subsequently introduced at the Kyiv Caves Lavra and became the foundational liturgical typikon throughout Kyivan Rus'.

[^195]: Sessional Hymn after the 2nd Kathisma at Matins, Tone 3, modeled after the prosomoion *Joseph marveled* (*Удивися Иосиф*).

[^196]: Sessional Hymn after the Polyeleos, Tone 5, modeled after *The Word co-beginningless* (*Собезначалное слово*). St. Ephraim's court title of *eunuch* (chamberlain and high steward to Grand Prince Izyaslav) is hymnographically interpreted in the spiritual sense of evangelical virginity and mortification of all carnal passions (cf. Matthew 19:12).

[^197]: Common Gospel lection for a Holy Hierarch (John 10:9–16), reading of the Good Shepherd.

[^198]: Canon to the Mother of God and to the Hierarch Ephraim, Tone 2; Ode 1 Heirmos: "Come, ye people, let us sing a song unto Christ God..." (Exodus 15:1–2).

[^199]: The *Kyiv Caves Paterikon* (Discourse 8) records that St. Antony received Ephraim the boyar and commanded the elder Nikon to tonsure him and vest him in the monastic habit (*schema*).

[^200]: Ode 3 Heirmos: "The bow of the mighty is broken..." (1 Samuel / 1 Kings 2:4).

[^201]: Ode 4 Heirmos: "I have heard, O Lord, Thy glorious dispensation..." (Habakkuk 3:2).

[^202]: Ode 5 Heirmos: "O Giver of light and Creator of the ages..." (Isaiah 26:9).

[^203]: Ode 6 Heirmos: "From the whale Jonah cried aloud unto the Lord..." (Jonah 2:2–9).

[^204]: Kontakion, Tone 8: commemorating St. Ephraim's historic achievement in transcribing the Studite Typikon at Constantinople and bringing it to St. Theodosius of the Caves for implementation across Rus'.

[^205]: Ode 7 Heirmos: "When the golden image was worshiped on the plain of Dura..." (Daniel 3:1–23).

[^206]: St. Ephraim of Pereyaslav was one of the senior hierarchs present at the solemn uncovering and translation of the incorrupt relics of St. Theodosius of the Caves from the cave to the Dormition Cathedral on August 14, 1091, as recounted in the Kyiv Caves Paterikon (Discourse 8) and the Primary Chronicle (sub anno 6599 [1091]).

[^207]: The Archangel Michael, Supreme Commander (*Archistrategos*) of the Heavenly Hosts. In 1089, Bishop Ephraim of Pereyaslav consecrated the great stone Cathedral of Saint Michael the Archangel in Pereyaslav, renowned for its architectural magnificence, frescoed walls, and accompanying stone bath-house and infirmary.

[^208]: Saint Prochor the Lebednik (Orache-Eater / Goosefoot-Eater), monk of the Kyiv Caves Monastery (d. February 10, 1107). During the severe famine in Rus' brought on by Polovtsian raids, he gathered wild orache (*Atriplex*, Church Slavonic *loboda*), rubbed it into flour with his hands, and baked loaves to feed the starving inhabitants of Kyiv; during the salt embargo imposed by Prince Svyatopolk Izyaslavich, he miraculously transformed wood ashes into pure salt. See Kyiv Caves Paterikon, Discourse 31.

[^209]: Melodic model: Tone 8 Heirmos: *The chariot-driver Pharaoh* (Колесницегонителя Фараона). Exod. 15:1–19.

[^210]: Melodic model: Tone 8 Heirmos: *O Lord, Who in the beginning established the heavens* (Утверждей в начале небеса). 1 Sam. (1 Kgs.) 2:2.

[^211]: Melodic model: Tone 8 Heirmos: *Thou art my strength, O Lord* (Ты моя крепость, Господи). Hab. 3:2.

[^212]: Melodic model: Tone 8 Heirmos: *Why hast Thou cast me off from Thy presence* (Вскую мя отринул еси). Ps. 42 (43):2.

[^213]: Melodic model: Tone 8 Heirmos: *Cleanse me, O Savior, for many are mine iniquities* (Очисти мя, Спасе). Jonah 2:3.

[^217]: Melodic model: Tone 8 Heirmos: *Heaven was amazed at this* (Ужасеся о сем небо).

[^218]: Saint Isaac the Recluse of the Caves (d. February 14, ca. 1090). Formerly a wealthy merchant of Toropets named Chern, he gave away his wealth to the poor and monasteries, came to the Kyiv Caves, and was tonsured by Saint Antony. He entered strict reclusion in a tiny cave cell; after seven years, the adversary appeared to him disguised as Christ surrounded by demons in angelic splendor. Deceived into prostrating before the demon, Isaac fell gravely ill and paralyzed for two years, lovingly nursed by Saints Antony and Theodosius. Upon his miraculous recovery, he adopted holy folly (*yurodstvo*), endured the severe cold of winter, and gained complete power over the demons. See Kyiv Caves Paterikon, Discourse 36.

[^219]: Melodic model: Tone 8 Podoben: *O most marvelous wonder* (О преславнаго чудесе).

[^220]: Melodic model: Tone 4 Heirmos: *The three captains of strength, O Thou born of the Virgin* (Тристаты крепкия). Exod. 15:4.

[^221]: Melodic model: Tone 4 Heirmos: *From the heights Thou camest down willingly upon earth* (Из высоты снизшел еси).

[^222]: St. Isaac's ascetic habit: after surviving demonic deception and recovering from two years of paralysis, he purchased an untanned goatskin, stitched it tightly over his coarse haircloth undergarment (*milot*), and let it shrink and dry onto his flesh, wearing it constantly without removal as an instrument of mortification. See Kyiv Caves Paterikon, Discourse 36.

[^223]: Melodic model: Tone 4 Heirmos: *For the sake of the love of Thine image, O Bountiful One* (Любве ради щедре твоего образа). Hab. 3:2–3.

[^226]: Kontakion to the Venerable Isaac the Recluse of the Caves, Tone 5, commemorating his victory over demonic delusion through voluntary self-abasement, holy folly (*yurodstvo*), and severe reclusion.

[^227]: Melodic model: Tone 4 Heirmos: *Deliver us not up unto the end for Thy name's sake* (Не предаждь нас до конца). Prayer of Azarias / Song of the Three Holy Children (Dan. 3:34–35, LXX).

[^229]: Melodic model: Tone 4 Heirmos: *All of us magnify Thy love for mankind, O Christ our Savior* (Величаем вси человеколюбие твое).

[^230]: Saint Eustratius the Faster and Venerable-Martyr of the Caves (d. March 28, 1097). Tonsured at the Kyiv Caves Monastery under St. Antony, he was renowned for strict fasting. In 1096, during a Polovtsian raid on Kyiv led by Bonyak, Eustratius was taken captive along with thirty monastic and lay companions and sold into slavery to a Jewish master in Chersonesus (Korsun). When their master attempted to force the Christian captives to renounce Christ, Eustratius exhorted them to endure starvation rather than apostatize; after all fifty captives died of hunger, the enraged master had Eustratius crucified upon a cross on the Paschal feast and pierced his side with a spear. See Kyiv Caves Paterikon, Discourse 29.

[^241]: Amalek, the ancient biblical enemy of Israel (Exod. 17:8–16), typifying spiritual warfare and demonic assault against monastics.

[^247]: The fifty Christian captives: according to Discourse 29 of the Kyiv Caves Paterikon, Eustratius was captured along with thirty of his own monastic and lay companions, while twenty other Christians were also taken, making fifty in all; all fifty died of hunger and thirst in the dungeon within fourteen days, having been exhorted by Eustratius to choose death rather than deny Christ.

[^255]: Byzantine Emperor Alexios I Komnenos (reigned 1081–1118). After the martyrdom of St. Eustratius, Emperor Alexios issued an imperial decree expelling and severely punishing Jewish slave-merchants in Chersonesus who held and persecuted Christian captives; the Jewish master who crucified Eustratius was executed by hanging upon a tree, fulfilling the saint's prophecy. See Kyiv Caves Paterikon, Discourse 29.

[^256]: Gen. 4:10 ("...the voice of thy brother's blood cries unto me from the ground").

[^257]: Saint Niphont, Bishop of Novgorod (d. April 8, 1156). Tonsured at the Kyiv Caves Monastery, he was consecrated Bishop of Novgorod in 1130. A staunch defender of canonical order, he refused to sanction the uncanonical marriage of Prince Svyatoslav Olgovich and opposed the uncanonical elevation of Clement Smoliatich as Metropolitan of Kyiv without the blessing of the Patriarch of Constantinople. Returning to the Kyiv Caves Monastery shortly before his death, he fell asleep in the Lord and was buried in the Near Caves of Saint Antony. See Kyiv Caves Paterikon, Discourse 14.

[^261]: The miraculous posthumous apparition of Saint Theodosius of the Caves to Bishop Niphont: shortly before his falling asleep, Bishop Niphont fell into a light sleep in the Caves Monastery and beheld Saint Theodosius holding an open scroll inscribed with the words of Isaiah: *Behold, I and the children whom God hath given me* (Isa. 8:18; Heb. 2:13), welcoming Niphont to eternal rest with the Caves brethren. See Kyiv Caves Paterikon, Discourse 14.

[^265]: In 1135 and 1147, Bishop Niphont personally intervened on the battlefield between the warring princes of Kyiv, Chernihiv, and Novgorod, courageously preventing bloodshed and reconciling the brothers in peace. See Novgorod First Chronicle and Kyiv Caves Paterikon, Discourse 14.

[^266]: 2 Tim. 4:2 ("Preach the word; be instant in season, out of season; reprove, rebuke, exhort with all longsuffering and doctrine").

[^267]: In 1136, Prince Svyatoslav Olgovich entered into an uncanonical marriage in Novgorod; Bishop Niphont refused to bless the union or allow his clergy to crown them, defying the prince's threats. See Novgorod First Chronicle.

[^269]: Saint Theodore the Studite (759–826), abbot of the Studios Monastery in Constantinople and champion of canonical order, who was exiled for resisting the adulterous marriage of Emperor Constantine VI (the "Moechian controversy"). Niphont is likened to him for withstanding both princes and uncanonical prelates.

[^270]: Clement Smoliatich, consecrated Metropolitan of Kyiv in 1147 by a synod of Rus' bishops without the sanction or blessing of Patriarch Nicholas IV Mouzalon of Constantinople, using the relic of the head of Saint Clement of Rome in place of patriarchal imposition of hands. Bishop Niphont of Novgorod firmly refused communion with Clement, citing the sacred canons that forbade setting up a metropolitan without the Oecumenical Patriarch.

[^271]: Melodic model: Tone 1 Heirmos: *The nethermost abyss hath compassed us* (Обыде нас последняя бездна). Jonah 2:5; Ps. 43:23 (44:22).

[^272]: In 1149, Grand Prince Izyaslav II Mstislavich of Kyiv, enraged by Niphont's refusal to recognize Metropolitan Clement, detained Niphont and confined him to the Kyiv Caves Monastery; when Prince Yuri Dolgorukiy took Kyiv in the autumn of 1149, he liberated Niphont and restored him to his see in Novgorod with great honor.

[^276]: Patriarch Luke Chrysoberges of Constantinople (served 1156–1169) sent an official patriarchal charter to Bishop Niphont, praising his steadfast orthodoxy and canonical discipline, comparing him to the ancient holy fathers and confessors. See Kyiv Caves Paterikon, Discourse 14.

[^277]: In early 1156, Bishop Niphont traveled to Kyiv to welcome the canonically consecrated Metropolitan Constantine I from Constantinople, but fell ill at the Kyiv Caves Monastery before Constantine's arrival and peacefully fell asleep in the Lord on April 8, 1156.

[^379]: Discourse 27 of the Kyiv Caves Paterikon relates that Agapitus never accepted any gold, silver, or gifts from those he healed, distributing everything to the poor.

[^380]: Heirmos of Ode 4, Tone 6 (Habakkuk 3:1–19).

[^381]: 1 Corinthians 1:27. Referring to the Armenian physician in Kyiv who was famous for diagnosing terminal illnesses by feeling a patient's pulse.

[^382]: Revelation 1:18. When the Armenian predicted that a sick boyar would die within eight days, Agapitus gave the boyar herbs from his cell and healed him.

[^383]: When the Armenian sent a man condemned to death to be given lethal poison, Agapitus gave him herbs and healed him, exposing the Armenian's plot.

[^384]: Mark 16:18: "And if they drink any deadly thing, it shall not hurt them." The Armenian tried to poison Agapitus himself, but Agapitus drank the poison unharmed.

[^385]: Heirmos of Ode 5, Tone 6 (Isaiah 26:9).

[^386]: Prince Vladimir Vsevolodovich Monomakh (1053–1125), then Prince of Chernihiv, fell mortally ill; the Armenian physician could not cure him, but Saint Agapitus sent him herbs from the Caves, and the prince was immediately healed.

[^387]: Vladimir Monomakh came in person to the Kyiv Caves Lavra to thank Agapitus and brought him rich gifts of gold and silver, but Agapitus refused to see him or accept any gifts.

[^388]: Heirmos of Ode 6, Tone 6 (Jonah 2:1–10).

[^389]: A boyar whom Agapitus healed brought him a large sum of gold; Agapitus threw it outside the door of his cell, refusing to keep any worldly wealth.

[^390]: Matthew 13:45–46; Philippians 3:8.

[^391]: When Saint Agapitus fell ill, the Armenian visited him, took his pulse, and stated he would die within three days, pledging to become an Orthodox monk if Agapitus survived; Agapitus told him the Lord revealed he would live three more months, which came to pass.

[^392]: Kontakion of Saint Agapitus of the Caves, Tone 6.

[^393]: Heirmos of Ode 7, Tone 6 (Daniel 3:49–50).

[^394]: Heirmos of Ode 8, Tone 6 (Daniel 3:57–88; 1 Kings / 1 Samuel 18:38).

[^395]: After Saint Agapitus reposed, he appeared in a vision to the Armenian physician, which brought about his conversion.

[^396]: The Armenian physician came to Hegumen John of the Caves, confessed his sins, asked for baptism and the monastic tonsure, and lived out his life as a monk in the Caves.

[^397]: Heirmos of Ode 9, Tone 6 (Luke 1:46–55).

[^493]: Genesis 39:7–20 (the chastity of Joseph in Egypt).

[^498]: St. Moses the Hungarian (Моисей Угрин, d. 1043), monk of the Kyiv Caves Monastery. In 1018, during the invasion of Kyiv by Duke Bolesław I the Brave of Poland on behalf of Sviatopolk the Accursed, Moses was captured along with his brother George and taken as a prisoner of war to Poland.

[^499]: Podoben "Joy of the heavenly ranks" (Небе́сныхъ чино́въ; Greek: Τῶν οὐρανίων ταγμάτων). Tone 1 automelon.

[^501]: Typological parallel to the Prophet Moses at Mount Horeb beholding the burning bush unconsumed (Exod. 3:2). In the hymnographer's mystical theology, the ascetic beholds God spiritually within the unburnt bush of his own pure soul.

[^502]: Podoben "O all-lauded martyrs" (Прехва́льнїи мꙋ́ченицы; Greek: Πανεύφημοι μάρτυρες). Tone 1 automelon.

[^503]: Holy Passion-bearer Prince Boris of Rus' (slain 1015 on the Alta River by the henchmen of Sviatopolk the Accursed). Moses was a member of Prince Boris's personal retinue and witness to his martyrdom.

[^505]: Psalm 149:5 (LXX).

[^506]: Allusion to the parable of the laborers in the vineyard (Matt. 20:1–16) who received each a denarius for their day's labor.

[^507]: The Polish noblewoman (a wealthy young widow) who purchased Moses from his captors, seeking to make him her husband and force him to break his vow of chastity, subjecting him to starvation, beatings, and castration upon his steadfast refusal.
